### BY JULIUS SPERBER <note>Julius Sperber (c. 1540–1616) was a German physician and counselor to the Prince of Anhalt. He is best known as a precursor to the Rosicrucian movement, blending Christian theology with alchemy and Kabbalah.</note> V. H. <note>original: "V. H." These initials likely represent a Latin title or status, such as "Verae Hermeticae" or a similar designation of his philosophical standing.</note>

---

->AMSTERDAM<-
->For Benedictus Bahnsen <note>Benedictus Bahnsen was a mid-17th-century publisher in Amsterdam known for printing the works of mystical, spiritualist, and "heretical" authors who faced censorship elsewhere in Europe.</note> / 1660.<-

<summary>This is the title page for "Mysterium Magnum" (The Great Mystery) by Julius Sperber, a mystical text exploring the nature of the Trinity and the human soul. Published in 1660, it reflects the flourishing of esoteric and spiritualist literature in Amsterdam during the 17th century.</summary>
<keywords>Julius Sperber, Mysterium Magnum, Secrets of God, Theology, Amsterdam Publishing, Benedictus Bahnsen, Rosicrucianism, Theosophy</keywords>

<image-desc>A decorative typographical header consisting of two rows of ornaments: the top row features stylized floral buds or leaves, and the bottom row features starburst or snowflake-like floral patterns.</image-desc>

## In this book is treated:

1. What **GOD** is in His essence. <note>The original German *Wesen* refers to the fundamental nature, being, or substance of God, a central concern of mystical theology.</note> Also
2. How the Son of God was begotten of the Father in a Divine manner.
3. How and what He is. Also
4. How He came into the world for the first time.
5. And in what form He shall appear on the <term>Last Day</term> <note>original: *Jüngsten Tage*; the day of final judgment at the end of the world.</note>.

### Likewise further:

1. What the soul, or the <term>inward invisible man</term> <note>A term used in Christian mysticism to distinguish the spiritual, eternal part of a human from the physical, "outward" body.</note> is in its essence.
2. How he was before the Fall.
3. How he is now, even after the Fall, in this earthly life.
4. And what his form is after death; also
5. How he shall regain his former glory in the life to come and what his form shall be.
6. What kind of new body he shall also receive,
7. From what materials the same [is composed], and
8. What its glory, state, and property shall be.

->Firstly, made known through Divine revelation: and then subsequently, through the guidance of Divine Heavenly Wisdom and the illumination of the Holy Spirit, sufficiently explained, proven, and confirmed from the written Word of God, according to its secret understanding.<-

<summary>This page provides a detailed table of contents for the volume, divided into two main sections: a Christological and theological study of God and the Son, and an anthropological and eschatological study of the human soul's journey from creation to the afterlife.</summary>

### *Augustine, Book 9, On the Trinity.*

In all knowledge, when we know God, a certain likeness of God is formed within us. <note>original: "In omni cognitione, cum Deum novimus, fit aliqua similitudo Dei in nobis." St. Augustine (354–430 AD) was a foundational Christian philosopher who argued that because humans are made in God's image, the structure of the human mind reflects the Holy Trinity.</note>

---

### *Macarius, Homily 7.*

Just as our eyes gaze upon the **SUN**: so also those who are enlightened see the Image of the soul, though few Christians see this. <note>original: "Quemadmodum oculi nostri aspiciunt SOLEM..." Macarius of Egypt was a 4th-century hermit; his homilies are a cornerstone of Eastern Christian mysticism, focusing on the "light" of the soul.</note>

---

### *Marsilio Ficino on Plato’s Phaedo,*
### *Chapter 7.*

The soul's **INNERMOST** and Divine form is known only to Divine beings. <note>original: "Animæ forma INTIMA & Divina, solis Divinis nota." Ficino (1433–1499) was a central figure of the Italian Renaissance who sought to harmonize Platonic philosophy with Christian theology.</note>

---

### *Bernard on the Song of Songs,*
### *Sermon 37.*

No one is saved without the knowledge of Self and of God, at least those who have the age and the faculty for knowing. Therefore, know Yourself, so that you may fear God: know Him, so that you may love Him just as much. In the one, you are initiated into Wisdom; in the other, you are perfected. For the fear of the Lord is the beginning of Wisdom, and the fullness of the law is <term>Charity</term> <note>original: *Charitas*; in this context, it refers to the highest form of Christian love—selfless, divine love for God and others.</note>. Thus, you must beware of ignorance of either, just as you cannot be saved without the fear and love of God.

---

### *The Same Place*

Therefore, when this twofold knowledge precedes within us, then whatever academic knowledge might happen to come upon us does not puff us up in the least: for such knowledge is able to bring no earthly benefit or honor that is not far inferior... <note>St. Bernard of Clairvaux (1090–1153) cautions that intellectual "knowledge" (*scientia*) is dangerous and prideful unless it is grounded in the "twofold knowledge" (*notitia*) of one's own soul and God.</note>

...with hope conceived, and with the joy of hope now more deeply rooted in the soul. <note>original: "spe conceptâ, lætitiâque ſpei jam altius radicatæ in animo." This completes the sentence from St. Bernard of Clairvaux on the previous page, describing the spiritual state of one who has begun to know God.</note>

### Augustine, *On the Utility of Believing.*

> No one doubts that all men are either Fools or Wise. However, I now call "Wise" not those who are merely shrewd and clever, but those who possess—as much as a human can—the most firmly perceived **KNOWLEDGE** of Man himself and of God, and whose life and character are in harmony with this knowledge. <note>original: "de utilitate credendi." St. Augustine (354–430 AD) argues here that true wisdom is not just intellectual "cleverness" but a deep realization of the relationship between the human and the divine that changes how one lives.</note>

### Augustine in the book *On Order*, Chapter 8.

> The inquiry of Philosophy is twofold. One part concerns the Soul; the other concerns God. The first causes us to know ourselves; the second, our origin. The former is sweeter to us; the latter is more precious. The former makes us worthy of a blessed life; the latter actually makes us blessed. The first is for those learning; the latter is for those already taught. This is the order of the Study of Wisdom, through which everyone becomes fit to understand the order of things. <note>original: "de Ordine." In this early work, Augustine explores how the study of the liberal arts and the soul prepares the mind to contemplate God, the source of all order.</note>

### Paracelsus in *The Sagacious Philosophy*, Book 1, Chapter 2.

> Regarding the origin of man, it is necessary to know from what he was made. And even though the ancient Philosophers do not mention this, nor do the Theologians, nor the Jurists, nor even the Physicians, this should not be counted as Wisdom on their part.
>
> For the Philosopher should first consider from where man comes, and then the other— <note>original: "Vom herkommen deß Menſchen iſt noth zu wiſſen..." Paracelsus (1493–1541) was a revolutionary physician and alchemist. Here he critiques traditional authorities for failing to understand the "materia" or spiritual substance from which humanity was created, which he believed was the foundation of all true science.</note>

<summary>This page presents a collection of philosophical and theological definitions of wisdom, arguing that true understanding requires a dual knowledge of the human soul and its divine origin. It marks a transition from the religious authority of St. Augustine to the natural philosophy of Paracelsus.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the passage from Paracelsus’s "Sagacious Philosophy" (also known as the *Astronomia Magna*). Paracelsus is arguing that all higher disciplines must be grounded in an understanding of the "cornerstone"—the fundamental nature and origin of humanity as a divine creation.</meta>

...lay the cornerstone. Accordingly, the **Theologian** <note>original: "Theologus"</note> should know this, so that he may know what the body is, what the mortal part <note>original: "tödtliche"</note> in him is, and what the eternal part is; this is so that he does not condemn the human being to the Devil. And the **Jurist** <note>Lawyer</note> should know this, so that he knows that Man is not a sow, but a noble **creature** <note>original: "Creatur"</note>; following this, he should know how to judge him as a human being, and not as if he were judging a calf. And the **Physician** <note>original: "Arzt"</note> should also know this beginning: so that he does not send the patient to the **slaughterhouse** <note>original: "Fleischbanck," literally a butcher’s stall or meat-bench</note> like his cattle, but rather considers the right of the divine **image** <note>original: "Bildnuß," referring to the *Imago Dei* or the belief that humans are created in the image of God</note> when deciding how to proceed with **medicine** <note>original: "Arzney"</note>.

For when one knows how Man is made, one also learns through that same knowledge to recognize his essence and his properties, which are the very same things from which he was created. <note>Paracelsus refers here to his "microcosm" theory: that the human being contains the same substances and spiritual forces found in the entire universe.</note>

Now they may judge and gain understanding as to what that substance is from which Man originates; for that substance is noble and held in high esteem among all people:

->It is only right that Man should<-
->be esteemed even more highly.<-

<summary>Paracelsus explains that theologians, lawyers, and physicians must understand the divine and noble nature of humanity to treat people with proper dignity rather than as mere animals. He concludes that because humans are made of a noble essence, they deserve the highest possible respect.</summary>

The only-begotten Son of God describes eternal life: that it is, or consists in this: that one knows God, and Him whom He has sent, Jesus Christ. This knowledge <note>original: "Erkäntniß"</note> of God, however, is of two kinds: <term>Theoretical</term> <note>original: "Theorica"; refers to knowledge gained through study or reasoning</note> or <term>Contemplative</term> <note>original: "Contemplativa"; refers to knowledge gained through prayerful meditation</note> and <term>Practical</term> <note>original: "Practica"</note>. No single human being can attain <term>practical knowledge</term> <note>original: "Practicam cognitionem"; here implying a complete, lived experience of the divine</note> in this earthly life, but rather only in the future eternal life. There, all the chosen of God shall behold God in His essence, as He is, face to face; and besides this, they shall also recognize Him in the highest perfection, essentially and actually. This recognition will be <term>truly a blessed life</term> <note>original: "verè vita beata"</note>, a most delightful, eternal, and blessed life. But just as God the Lord is in Himself the highest good, so also here in this life the right, proper, and true knowledge of God—though we can only have it <term>theoretically</term> <note>original: "theoricè"</note> or <term>contemplatively</term> <note>original: "contemplativè"</note>—is the supreme <term>knowledge</term> <note>original: "Cognition"</note>, understanding, and science which an enlightened

<margin>Cicero, Book 3, *On the Nature of the Gods*.</margin>
Among the philosophers, **Simonides** <note>A Greek lyric poet (c. 556–468 BC) famous for his wisdom; Cicero recounts how Simonides asked for more time to define God, eventually concluding the task was impossible</note>—when he had attempted to sharply investigate the **Divine Essence** and what God is using his own reason—finally found that such a thing was impossible for him. He realized that the longer he tried, the less he could know of it; indeed, he had to confess that the more he searched into this high matter of God and His essence, the more obscure and hidden it appeared to him.

I, too, must certainly acknowledge this for myself: for it is not possible for any mortal human—however wise, witty, or sharp-minded they may be—to ever, in all eternity, fathom, speculate upon, or research even the smallest detail of God and His **Divine Essence**, nor even of the **human soul**, through their own human thoughts, reason, and <term>subtlety</term> <note>original: "Spißfindigkeit"; refers to overly-refined or hair-splitting logic</note>. <margin>Isaiah 55:8–9.</margin>
> My thoughts are not your thoughts, and my ways are not your ways, says the LORD. For as much as the heavens are higher than the earth, so are my ways higher than your ways, and my thoughts than your thoughts.

Through this answer, all that was cited above regarding the attainment of <term>knowledge</term> <note>original: "Wissenschafft"; here meaning a systematic or certain understanding of a subject</note> of such high and divine matters is quite neatly and correctly limited and explained, namely to this effect: that while it is true that no mortal man can, by himself and out of his own brain, fathom or reach the knowledge of such exceedingly high things, it is possible if such things are **revealed** and made known to him by God and His Holy Spirit through divine wisdom.

->PREFACE.<-

...many times inwardly called upon and prayed <note>The author is continuing a sentence from the previous page, describing his persistent prayer.</note> that, if it were His divine will, He would graciously reveal and manifest to me, through His Spirit, the mystery of the human soul—its essence, true origin, form, and condition—which is hidden from so many people. I was finally, at this time, granted this supplication of mine by the merciful God in a fatherly manner, and even more than I had asked for. For the Lord God most graciously revealed to me not only what the soul of man is, but also what God Himself is in His essence. Also, so that I might more easily grasp such high, supernatural, and great mysteries with my **dim and weak understanding** <note>In this period, "dull" or "dim" (blöde) referred to the inherent limitations of human intellect compared to divine light, rather than a lack of intelligence.</note>, He miraculously prefigured them for me through the **parables** <note>original: "Gleichnüssen"; can also mean "likenesses" or "analogies."</note> of earthly, visible, and tangible things.

[...have] similarly held them against the **touchstone** <term>touchstone (Probierstein): a metaphor for a standard of measurement, derived from the stone used to test the purity of gold and silver</term> of **Holy Divine Scripture**. * And I have found them, in all points and circumstances, not only not contrary to it, but entirely uniform, correct, and true. > [Things gathered from the Holy Scriptures are to be held just as if they were written in them. The Order of Preachers <note>The Dominicans</note>, in everything they learned through spiritual revelation, consulted the sayings of the ancient fathers; and they believed a thing had finally been revealed to them by the Lord only when they recognized that it differed in no way from what they read in Holy Scripture. Thus writes Gregory <note>Pope Gregory I (the Great), c. 540–604 AD.</note> in Book 3 of his *Exposition on 1 Kings, Chapter 3*.]

In all of which I was then able to sense the teaching and instruction of the Spirit of God all the more clearly: because the same—

The condition of human **SOULS** is altogether marvelous and pitiable: although they perceive so many things outside themselves through the liveliness of their intellect, yet they are unable by any perspicacity to know or contemplate **THEMSELVES** (as they are). ## Summary Content of the Chapters of this Treatise.

**Chapter I.** Summary content and outline of this entire treatise / concerning **GOD** / his Son / and the soul of man.
**II.** Whether one may see or know God in this life?
**III.** What God is.
**IV.** That God is in fire / and commonly appears in a fiery form.
**V.** Of the nature and properties of fire in general.
**VI.** What kind of fire **God the Lord** is?
**VII.** That even the highest heaven / and thus God's throne and dwelling, is fiery?
**VIII.** How even through elemental fire the Trinity of God is to some extent prefigured for us.
**IX.** Of light / its nature and properties.
**X.** That the fire / which is God / is an exceedingly bright and clear light.

## Chapter 1.

### Summary Contents and Outline of this entire Treatise <term>treatise: a formal and systematic written discourse on a subject</term> on God, His Son, and the Soul of Man.

<margin>
See ch. 4, 5, 6, 7, 8, ch. 9, 10, 11, 12, 13.
</margin>
God the Lord is, in His divine essence <term>essence: the fundamental nature or "being" of God</term>, a great fire and an exceedingly bright light—so bright and clear that it cannot be looked upon by any physical or fleshly eye, <margin>ch. 2.</margin> nor can any physical creature approach or come near Him. <margin>ch. 3.</margin> This same Fire and Light is pure life and self-sustaining being, which takes its beginning, origin, and maintenance from nothing else; rather, it is that from which all other living creatures receive their being and life. And just as God is in Himself the *greatest fire* <note>original: "Ignis maximus"</note>, the *Father of Lights* <note>original: "Pater Luminum"</note>, and the *Fountain of life* <note>original: "Fons vitæ"</note>: <margin>ch. 14, 15, 16.</margin> so He has, first of all, caused a ray of light or radiance to come forth or flow from and out of Himself—out of His divine Fire, Light, and Living Essence. Indeed, He has brought forth <note>original: "eructiret," from the Latin word for a heart overflowing with speech</note> from His heart a *fiery word* <note>original: "verbum ignitum"</note>, a word of life, the light of life, a true

### Summary Content.

...burned and shone in the first human as a most brilliant flame of light: it possesses within and of itself the *power of propagating, multiplying, and living* <note>original: "virtutem propagandi, multiplicandi, & vivendi"</note>. For man was originally created by God so that he should remain eternally and unchangeably united in body and soul in this way. He would have remained in that same state, form, and shape, and his soul would have persisted in that first bright and clear-flaming radiance of light, had man himself not changed through the Fall of Sin <note>German: *Sünden-fall*</note>, acting against the express prohibition of God. 

For through this change wrought by man himself, the substance of the body became dark and entirely gloomy in itself, whereby the flame of the soul was also significantly clouded, obscured, and darkened. Consequently, the soul can no longer be seen through the darkened body, nor can the soul itself see other souls, flames, or spirits of its own kind because of the darkened, gloomy body with which it is surrounded on all sides. Only the eyes were left to it in the darkened body as transparent glasses, through which it alone may properly behold tangible, earthly, and physical things. 

However, before the Fall of man, the union of the body and the soul was in itself heavenly, constant, and inextinguishable; the body was indestructible, and the soul-flame was made and ordained by God to be unquenchable. But now, because man has changed and corrupted himself through the Fall,

### Summary Content.

Through this, the soul in the man (which lies hidden within him in an invisible way, like fire in a flint-stone) once again gives off such glowing little sparks and, as it were, sprays them out; these are then united with a small **mass** <note>original: "massa"</note> of human flesh and blood (in which they also **lie hidden** <note>original: "latitiren"</note> alongside the astral spirit, like a spark of fire in the ashes).

## Chapter II.

->Whether one may see or at least recognize God in this life.<-

<margin>Augustine, *Confessions*, Book 2</margin>
St. Augustine writes: that man was made and created for this purpose, that he should see and recognize God his Creator. Because this is so poorly understood in itself, it cannot be interpreted or applied to anyone but man as he was before the **Fall** <note>original: "Fall," referring to the biblical Fall of Man in the Garden of Eden</note>. For at the present time, and ever since the Fall, the mind and understanding of man are so darkened that he cannot behold God with physical eyes.

<margin>Exodus 33:20</margin>
Therefore, when Moses desired to see God, He gave him this answer: he could not behold His face, for it was impossible for a man to see God in this life.

<margin>Sirach 43:35</margin>
Therefore the wise man **Sirach** <note>original: "Syrach," refers to the author of the deuterocanonical Book of Sirach or Ecclesiasticus</note> also says: Who has seen God, that he could speak of Him? Just as later in the New Testament the Lord Christ Himself says, and the Evangelist John writes:

<margin>
John 5:37 & chapter 6:46.
1 John 1:18 & chapter 4:12.
1 Timothy 6:16.
</margin>
That no one has ever seen God. And the Apostle Paul also reports *that no man has seen God; indeed, that it is not even possible for a man to see Him.*

Now, whether it is true that we [see] God **simply** <note>original: "simpliciter," a Latin term meaning purely, directly, or without qualification</note>, and as He is in and of him-

<summary>This page begins the second chapter by arguing that, due to the Fall of Man, humans are currently unable to see God with their physical eyes or fully comprehend Him. It cites St. Augustine and various biblical authorities like Moses, Sirach, Paul, and John to establish the impossibility of the beatific vision in this earthly life.</summary>
<keywords>St. Augustine, Moses, Sirach, John the Evangelist, Apostle Paul, the Fall of Man, Creator, New Testament, divine vision, spiritual blindness</keywords>

# Summary Content.

...self, and love and honor His Son. All of which will bring them (the elect <term>the elect: those chosen by God for salvation</term>) such an exceeding, unceasing, and insatiable joy that it cannot be grasped at all by our current weak and dull reason <note>original: "blöden Vernunfft"; in this period, "blöd" meant weak, infirm, or dim-sighted rather than its modern derogatory meaning</note>, much less described or spoken with fleshy human tongues.

On the other hand, the spirits of the damned will be kept smoldering <margin>chapter 36.</margin> until the day of judgment. Then, through the righteous and strict judgment and verdict of Christ <margin>chapter 36.</margin>, they will be completely and utterly blotted out, extinguished, and cast into eternal darkness unto the second and eternal death. There they shall be entirely deprived of the vision of God and all the glory of the elect for all eternity, and thus be completely separated or severed from God. And instead of the eternal joy that the elect of God feel in the kingdom of heaven, they will have an eternal howling and gnashing of teeth together in the abyss of dark Hell. This will be such an exceedingly great and unbearable painful torment, anguish, and fierce agony <note>original: "Pein, Angst und grimmige Qual"</note>, which likewise cannot be grasped or spoken by any earthly man in this world.

And what has now been told summarily <note>original Latin: "summariter"</note> in this first chapter shall be grandly, thoroughly, and extensively proven in the following chapters from the written Word of God.

<summary>This page concludes the summary of the first chapter, contrasting the incomprehensible joy of the elect in heaven with the eternal, agonizing separation of the damned in hell. It serves as a transition, promising that these claims will be proven through scripture in the chapters to follow.</summary>

Whether One May See God.

C iiij

## The Second Chapter.

->Whether one may see<-
->or at least recognize God<-
->in this life.<-

<margin>Augustine, Confessions, Book 2</margin>
St. Augustine writes: that man was made and created for this purpose, that he should see and recognize God his Creator. Because this statement is so plainly understood <note>original: "schlecht verstanden"; in this period, "schlecht" meant "simply" or "plainly" rather than its modern meaning of "badly"</note> in itself, it can be interpreted and applied in no other way than to man as he was before the Fall <term>the Fall: the biblical event in which humanity lost its original innocence</term>. For at the present time, and since the Fall, man’s senses and understanding are so greatly darkened that he cannot look upon God with physical eyes.

<margin>Exodus 33:20</margin>
Therefore, when Moses desired to see God, God gave him this answer: He could not look upon His face; for it was impossible for a man to see God in this life.

<margin>Sirach 43:35</margin>
Hence the wise man Sirach also says: Who has seen God, that he might speak of Him? Just as later in the New Testament, the Lord Christ himself says, and the Evangelist John writes:

<margin>John 5:37 & chapter 6:46; John 1:18 <note>the original OCR says "1 Joh. 1.18," but likely refers to the Gospel of John 1:18</note> & chapter 4:12; 1 Timothy 6:16</margin>
That no one has ever seen God. And the Apostle Paul also reports * that no man has seen God, indeed, that it is not even possible for a man to see Him.

Now, whether it is true that we see God simply <term>simpliciter: a Latin term meaning "absolutely" or "directly," referring to seeing God's essence without mediation</term>, and as He is in and of

<meta>This page continues the discussion from the previous page regarding the visibility of God. <header>Whether one [may see] God in this [life]</header>

is in itself / impossible to gaze upon: yet we may nonetheless see Him in a certain respect <note>original: "secundum quid"; a Latin scholastic term meaning "partially" or "in a certain regard" as opposed to seeing something in its entirety</note>.

<margin>Augustine in the book *On Faith*.</margin>
Augustine explains this seeing in this way: namely, that we can see God the **Lord** through the understanding, yet we can by no means comprehend Him.

<margin>2 Corinthians 2:14.</margin>
Therefore, the Apostle Paul very artfully calls such a seeing—the science or recognition (which one can have of God and His essence)—a fragrance of the knowledge of God <note>original: "odorem notitiæ Dei"</note>, which is to say, only a scent of the knowledge of God. However, this is not intended to mean that every person without distinction can perceive God through such a scent, or gaze upon or recognize Him with their understanding. Rather, in the places just mentioned, Augustine and Paul speak only of those whose understanding and the eyes of whose hearts are opened and enlightened by the Spirit of God, so that they may recognize God and His great mystery.

<margin>Gregory, *Moralia*, Book 13.</margin>
Therefore Gregory writes that in this life, enlightened people see the mysteries of the divine nature as if through a mental image <note>original: "imagination und Einbildung"; in this context, it refers to a conceptual or spiritual representation rather than a literal physical sight</note>. 

<margin>Anselm, *On the Gospel*.</margin>
And another Teacher of the Church writes that the creature cannot see or know God the Creator in any other way than as far as He wishes to reveal Himself to them. 

<margin>Hilary, *On the Trinity*, Book 5.</margin>
Thus the ancient teacher Hilary has also written on this subject with this opinion: We must learn from God what is to be understood about God, for He is not known except through His own authorship <note>original: "A Deo discendum quid de Deo intelligendum sit : quia non nisi se authore cognoscitur"</note>. And Bernard likewise maintains that one [must recognize] the

<margin>When speech happens to fail and cannot express (what it strives for) with integrity, then it is more fitting for us to glorify God: for we have such a God who transcends both Understanding and the Intuition of thought. Now, although I for my part have also received from the Spirit of God such a high revelation as a **fragrance of the knowledge of God** <note>original: "odorem notitiæ Dei"</note>; yet in a human way, and in this temporal life, it is not possible for me to express this exceedingly great mystery with my mortal tongue as it deserves, nor to describe it sufficiently. It is just as impossible as it would be for a person who smelled a physical herb or scent to properly describe that same fragrance, or to properly and sufficiently tell of such a lovely scent with words to another who did not hold it before their own nose and perceive the scent themselves. Therefore, Augustine’s* opinion is certainly correct: **that God is more truly thought of than He is spoken of** <note>original: "quod Deus veriùs cogitetur, qvam dicatur"</note>. That is, that one can think of God more truly and better in one’s understanding than one can properly speak of Him or express anything about Him with words.

He would not be everywhere, <margin>1 Corinthians 8:6.</margin> He would not be all in all, and above all things. <header>Concerning the Nature of Fire.</header>

receive from fire their color or visible form.

<margin>*Aristotle, Meteorology, Book 4; 2 Esdras 4, 5.*</margin>
*Aristotle* also writes that all other elements are subject to decay <note>original: "Fäulung" (putrefaction); in ancient science, fire was the only element that did not rot or spoil.</note>, yet fire alone does not decay. Likewise, fire cannot be weighed. In summary, fire possesses such a strange nature and property that even the ancients could not properly understand it; rather, it was entirely dark and unknown to them. From this, it undoubtedly arose that many of the most prominent among the heathens (especially the Persians, Assyrians, and Medes) held the element of fire to be a god, and rendered it the greatest and most distinguished honor.

<margin>*Quintus Curtius, Book 3, The Deeds of Alexander the Great.*</margin>
Hence the Romans also kept a fire in the Temple of *Vesta* (which was lit by the sun) as a heavenly treasure for their religious worship. We wish here in this place to describe fire in a new way: that in itself it is a subtle, invisible spirit of hot and dry quality, which cannot be seen by material eyes steadily and continuously on its own, unless it unites with or is taken up by a material body <note>original: *Corpore*</note>. When that happens, one can very clearly and easily see the material body <note>original: *Corpus materiale*</note> and the spiritual and incomprehensible body <note>original: *Corpus*</note> of the fire as distinct things. When the spirit of fire has thus united with a material body, it then consumes that body until it is entirely...

The author defines fire as an invisible, "subtle" spirit that only becomes visible when it inhabits and consumes material objects.</summary>

consumed. And when the union comes to an end through the consumption of the body <note>original: *Corporis*</note>, then the spirit of fire is no longer seen by material eyes. The ancient teacher Cyril <note>Cyril of Alexandria (c. 376–444 AD), a Patriarch of Alexandria who wrote extensively on the nature of Christ and the Trinity.</note> writes concerning GOD in this manner: *God, as soon as He is [perceived], is at once Fire, and Heat, and Light.* <note>original Latin: "Deus simul atque factus est Ignis, & Calor & Lumen est."</note> This is as if he were to say: If I am to describe God, how and what He is, He is at once a fire, a warmth, and a light. For elemental fire <note>referring to physical fire as one of the four classical elements (earth, air, fire, water)</note> also possesses these three things: it is fire in itself; it provides warmth; and it gives off light and radiance. In the same way, Augustine <note>Augustine of Hippo (354–430 AD), one of the most influential Latin Church Fathers.</note> likewise compares the Holy Trinity to fire. And once again, he describes fire according to these three properties: namely, that it possesses first, motion; second, radiance or light; and third, heat or warmth.

In this regard, I must also mention the threefold radiance of a burning candle or light. For if one pays close attention to the flame itself, one will see not only the elongated flame of the light itself, but also a round greenish glow surrounding the flame, and again, a reddish glow surrounding the outside of that. ## Chapter XI.

### That God, because He is a light, can to some extent be compared to the Sun as an astral <term>astral: relating to the stars or the celestial spheres; in this context, it refers to the highest form of physical light in the created universe</term> light.

Although God the Lord—as has been set forth above—is a light, yet this light cannot be properly described exactly as it is in itself. However, so that the same may nevertheless be made known to mankind to some degree, this divine light has been compared to the Sun as an *astral light*. This is not because it is equal to the Sun in all things, but for these reasons: because the Sun (according to the unanimous opinion of the philosophers <note>original: "Philosophorum"</note>) is also fiery, and thus is an astral fire; but primarily because there is otherwise no visible light (that mortal or human eyes can behold which gives off such an exceedingly great shine and brilliance) except for the Sun.

This is intended not <term>qualitatively: "qualitativè"; referring to a temporary or accidental attribute</term>, but <term>essentially: "essentialiter"; referring to the core nature or substance of a thing</term>, as the <term>logicians: "Logici"; scholars of formal reasoning and dialectic</term> call it.

[of the truth] of God, a ray of the brightness of the Almighty, and a radiance of the eternal Light.

Now, whoever reads and looks upon such words according to common understanding, and how the bare letter sounds, without the addition and explanation of other passages, will never be able to understand what the prophet means by them. But if one considers here what was cited above concerning God (namely, that he is a Fire and a Light), and reflects properly on the matter according to the wisdom of God and the hidden understanding of Holy Scripture: then one finds from such words as much as if the prophet had intended to say and teach that the Messiah was a **brand plucked from that fire which is God himself** <note>original Latin: "torris de illo igne, qui est Deus ipse, erutus"</note>.

# How the Son [proceeds] from the Father

<margin>
Wis. 5:17.
Isa. 52:10.
Deut. 4:34 & elsewhere.
Jer. 27:5.
Ibid. 32:17.
</margin>
...[called the] **outstretched arm of God** <note>original Latin: "Dei extensum"</note>, in which God has made and created all things.

<margin>
* Theodoret, Book 5 in the *Letter on Divine Matters*, chapter on the Son.
</margin>
Because an somewhat obscure mention of this exceedingly great mystery (how the Light is generated from Light) occurs in Holy Scripture, some **Christian Fathers** <note>A term for early influential theologians of the first few centuries of the Church.</note> and Church Teachers have written about it all the more clearly.

<margin>
Tertullian in *Apology*, ch. 21.
Cyril, Book 1 of the *Thesaurus against heretics*, ch. 8.
Lactantius. Also Justin, Book 4, ch. 29.
</margin>
In particular, the ancient teacher Cyril writes of this in these words: **"But the Son is in the Father, just as the radiance of the sun is in the sun, and proceeds from it, yet is one in nature; for he is the seal and the most perfect image of his nature itself"** <note>original Latin: "Est autem in Patre Filius, sicut est splendor Solis in sole, & procedit ab ipso, sed unum naturâ: sigillum namque & perfectissima imago naturæ ipsius est."</note>. And again: **"The Father begot the Son from himself as light and radiance"** <note>original Latin: "Pater Filium ex se ipso, ut lucem atque splendorem genuit."</note>.

<margin>
Basil, Book 2 *Against Eunomius*.
</margin>
And Basil says: **"The Son shows the Father entirely in himself, shining forth from the total majesty of the Father"** <note>original Latin: "Filius totum in se ipso Patrem ostendit, ex tota majestate Patris resplendens."</note>.

<margin>
Augustine, *On Time*, Sermon 10, for the Vigil of Pentecost; see also his *On the Creed to the Catechumens*, Book 1, ch. 3, where he says: "From the moment the Fire began TO BE, it followed that the radiance was begotten."
</margin>
Likewise, Augustine also writes about this as follows: **"God is not to be debated by us, but believed. But, to speak briefly, it suffices for us to know that the light begot the radiance"** <note>original Latin: "Non à nobis discutiendus est Deus, sed credendus. Sed, ut breviter dicamus, sufficit nobis scire, quia genuit lux splendorem."</note>. Which we have intentionally omitted to translate into German <note>The original 17th-century author left this quote in Latin out of reverence, fearing a vernacular translation might fail to capture the precise theological nuance.</note> for this reason: so that we might do neither too little nor too much to the matter. However, the aforementioned Church Teachers generally intended to indicate this: that the Son of God is the **begotten light** <note>original Latin: "lux genita"</note>, begotten from an **unbegotten light** <note>original Latin: "à luce ingenita"</note>; and is thus a shine or brilliance which goes out from the Father-Light. Of this, the Prophet Isaiah wrote in the Old Testament: **"Until he goes forth as radi-"** <note>The text breaks mid-word, citing Isaiah 62:1.</note>

That such fatherhood is primarily directed toward the **rational soul** <note>The "rational soul" (Latin: anima rationalis) was traditionally considered the part of the human being that possesses intellect and immortality, distinct from the biological life force.</note> of man appears all the more from this: that not only in the Old Testament the prophet Isaiah, but much more so in the New Testament the apostle Paul, makes an explicit distinction between our bodily fathers (namely, our earthly parents) and our only spiritual Father, who is God the Lord. The Latin text expresses this mystery in even clearer words, calling our bodily parents *fathers of the flesh* <note>original Latin: "Patres carnales"</note> but God the Lord the *Father of Spirits* <note>original Latin: "Patrem Spirituum" (specifically: of our spirits)</note>. This is so that one may understand that, just as we have both body and soul—or spirit—within us, so too we have two different kinds of fathers for these things. Namely, the fathers of our flesh are our parents, but the only Father of our spirits or souls is God the Lord. As was established above, He is called a Father of the spirits of all flesh, and we are His, God’s children.

...ther <note>The text begins with the second half of the word "Father" (German: *Vater*), completing the biblical reference to calling upon God as "Abba, Father."</note>; the same Spirit gives testimony to our spirit that we are God’s children. From these things, we now see that in being Christ’s brothers, we have much more cause to rejoice and boast in His brotherhood because of our divine nature and origin <note>original: *Ankunfft*, referring to the soul's descent or ancestry from God</note> than because of our human kinship, in that Christ took on our flesh. <margin>2 Corinthians 5:16</margin>Regarding this, the aforementioned Apostle explicitly says: "Therefore, from now on we know no one according to the flesh; and though we have known Christ according to the flesh, yet now we know Him so no longer." Just as it was first explained regarding God above in Chapter 4: That He is a **Fire** and **Light**; likewise we will prove and demonstrate below in Chapter 20 that the rational soul of man is similarly a **fiery light**. Second, just as God is a **Spirit**, so too the rational soul of man is a pure spirit. Third, just as God is (or in God is) the **source of life**, so too the soul is called elsewhere the *breath of life* <note>original: *Spiraculum vitæ*</note>, or "Life" itself; as the Wise Man <note>Refers to Solomon, the traditional author of Proverbs.</note> also says: That life comes out of the heart. Fourth, as it is written of God that He is **all in all**, so too the soul is called "the soul, as if it were all things" <note>original: *anima quasi OMNIA*</note>. Concerning which *Augustine* writes that it bears the image of all things within itself. Fifth, just as in the Word of God there is no distinction, but it is one thing—the **Son of GOD**, and the Word of the Father, and the radiance of glory—even so the substance of the soul is, in its own way, also **spiritual and simple** <note>original: *spiritualis & simplex*; "simple" here means not made of parts, indivisible.</note>, without any distinction of the senses. Sixth, as God is the **life of the soul**, so conversely the soul is the **life of the body**. Seventh, just as the Lord God is **invisible** in Himself, so too the soul of man is invisible; for which reason the Apostle Peter calls it the **hidden man of the heart**. Eighth, as God is and remains **eternal**, so too the soul of man—

## Chapter XXI.
### That through Elemental Fire the Soul of Man can be, in a Natural Manner, Somewhat Portrayed and Compared.

---

That the human soul possesses such a nature, quality, and condition can (in addition to what has been cited and set forth sequentially above from the Word of God) also be irrefutably proven and demonstrated according to nature. For although the rational soul is not an **elemental fire** <note>the physical fire found in the natural world</note>, nor blown into man from the **elemental air**, nor (like the **astral spirit** <term>the *spiritus astralis* or "star-spirit," believed to be a subtle intermediary between the physical body and the eternal soul</term>) derived from the fire and the stars, but rather originates from the living and **essential fire** <note>fire as a divine substance or "essence"</note> (which is God Himself) and is breathed in by the **Spirit of God**; nevertheless—as we have set forth above—because God Himself allows Himself to be compared in several things to elemental fire: so also can and may the soul of man (otherwise an invisible thing in itself) be all the more compared in several things with visible and elemental fire. Thus, through the form and properties of fire, the soul’s own forms, types, and shapes, as well as its condition...

There have been various opinions among the pagan philosophers. **Plato** and **Democritus** believed that the soul has its seat in the whole head. **Strabo** claimed it was between the eyebrows; **Erasistratus** <note>A Greek anatomist and royal physician</note> placed it around the cerebellum <note>original: *Hirnhäuptlein*, literally the "little brain"</note>; **Hippocrates** and **Galen** located it in the brain; **Herophilus** <note>original: *Hierophylus*, an early Alexandrian physician</note> in the cavity or the lower part of the brain; **Xenocrates** in the crown of the head; **Parmenides** (as well as the **Epicureans** and **Egyptians**) in the whole chest; **Empedocles** in the substance of the blood; **Moschion** in the entire body; and so forth—one held this opinion, while another held that.

<margin>4 Esdras 16:62 <note>An apocryphal book of the Bible</note></margin>

<margin>Proverbs 4:23</margin>

<margin>Bede's Commentary on Mark; Jerome on Matthew chapter 15, and book 2 [of his letters] to Fabiola on the priestly vestments.</margin>
**Bede** and **Jerome** also write based on such grounds, rejecting the pagan opinion, as follows: The soul's primary place or seat is not in the brain, according to the opinion of **Plato**, but rather according to the teaching of **Christ**, it is in the heart. <margin>Psalm 22:27</margin>

# Chapter XXIII.

->That the soul of man is<-
->propagated and multiplied through the<-
->intercourse of man and woman.<-

This question—namely, whether the soul of man is propagated from the parents to the children, or whether it is specifically infused by God at each and every conception—has been highly controversial until now, not only among <term>Philosophers</term> <note>Scholars of logic and natural science</note>, but also among <term>Theologians</term> <note>Scholars of religious doctrine</note>. <margin>Jerome on Ecclesiastes, chapter 12.</margin> The ancient teacher **Jerome** writes that those are rightly to be laughed at who believe the soul is propagated along with the body, and thus is not generated by God, but by the physical parents. <margin>Augustine, book 3 of *On Free Choice of the Will*.</margin> Saint **Augustine** also (having previously disputed at length in his writings <term>on the incarnation of the soul</term> <note>original: *de incarnatione animae*; here referring to the origin and "clothing" of the soul in flesh</note>), first considers whether all other souls were further multiplied and pro-
pagi- <note>The word "propagated" is split here for the page turn</note>

<summary>This page concludes the argument that the heart is the biological and spiritual center of man, then begins a debate on Traducianism—the idea that souls are inherited from parents—versus Creationism.</summary>
<keywords>heart, biology, Aristotle, Jerome, Augustine, soul's origin, propagation, traducianism, conception</keywords>

--- Page 97 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 90 regarding the origin of the human soul. The catchword "pagi-" from the previous page concludes here in the word "pagiret" (propagated), as the author weighs three historical theories on how the soul enters the body.</meta>

...is propagated. <note>original: *pagiret*, a Latinate form of 'propagated', continuing the thought from the previous page</note> Secondly: or whether each soul specifically is made in a new way daily? Thirdly: or whether all souls were prepared beforehand and are in a certain place, from where they are either sent by God into the bodies or enter into them voluntarily? <margin>Augustine, *On the Origin of the Soul*.</margin> He finally concludes elsewhere that souls are made daily by God, following the example of the first man. This opinion was of such pressing and great seriousness to him in that place that he even <margin>Augustine, Letter 16, at the end of Volume 2.</margin> anathematizes <note>To formally curse or excommunicate; a severe ecclesiastical condemnation</note> and curses those who believe otherwise. However, he is so uncertain of this fixed opinion that a good while later, in another <margin>Also *On the Origin of the Soul*, Book 1, Chapters 15 and 16.</margin> place, he explicitly writes of himself that this dispute—namely, whether the soul is propagated by the parents or must be daily created and breathed in by God—has not yet been correctly decided or brought to certainty. <margin>Augustine, the aforementioned Letter 28.</margin> 

Therefore, he only later writes to **Jerome** and desires to know from him, quite industriously and diligently, which of these opinions is right and true. Yet, the aforementioned **Jerome** <margin>Jerome, Letters 32 and 28 to Anastasia.</margin> also still doubted such things and their certainty himself, just as the ancient teacher **Origen** was in such doubt <margin>Origen, Book 1 of *On First Principles* <note>original: *Periarch*, likely referring to *Peri Archon*. The margin notes *fortè Hierarch*, suggesting a potential alternative title like *Ecclesiastical Hierarchy*</note>.</margin>.

Since they have not been able to reach final certainty on this point, even to this day our <term>Philosophers</term> <note>Scholars of natural law and logic</note> and <term>Theologians</term> <note>Scholars of scripture and divinity</note> remain (if not entirely) in doubt, and are certainly not so sure of their established opinions:

# That the Soul of Man

the LORD, as a Master over all masters, has shown a great masterpiece in this very matter: that He once created such a soul and breathed it into the first human—a soul which has within itself such a **power of multiplying and propagating** <note>original: *Virtutem multiplicandi & propagandi*</note> that thereafter, **successively**, many thousands of thousands of souls can be propagated and, as it were, **generated** from it. For this reason, the human soul is also quite aptly called by the <margin>Malachi 2:15</margin> prophet **Malachi** the **Seed of God** <note>original: *Semen Dei*</note>, a seed which GOD Himself planted into man, which is of such a nature that it continually provides its own seed further. For thus He says: **Did not the One make them? (Namely, husband and wife?) And is the remnant of His Spirit His? Deus) quærit nisi semen Dei?*</note> <margin>Guard</margin> **Therefore guard your Spirit, etc.** <note>original: *Custodite ergò Spiritum vestrum*</note> The highly enlightened <margin>1 Cor. 11:12</margin> Apostle **Paul** looked toward this same point when he wrote: as the woman comes from the man, so also the man comes through the woman, but all things come from GOD. And when the wise man speaks of **Adam’s** descendants, he says: they all have <margin>Wisdom 7:6</margin> one entrance into life, and a like departure.

For since God the Lord breathed a soul into the first man, Adam, that soul also possessed a reproductive power and quality, so that it would not further be necessary for God to give or breathe a specific soul into every single person. Instead, He commanded humanity to grow and multiply or increase. This *power of propagating and multiplying* <note>original Latin: *Virtus propagandi & multiplicandi*</note> is certainly not found in the substance of the destructible, mortal, and perishable flesh, but in the soul of the human being. 

For if God still, as happened in the very beginning, gave or breathed a soul into every person individually, then one could not rightly or properly say that this father had begotten a son in his own <term>image</term> <note>German: *Ebenbilde*; refers to the *Imago Dei* or the likeness of the parent being passed to the child.</note>, as the Scripture says of Adam in <margin>Gen. 5:3</margin>: that he begot a son who was like his own image. ## That the Soul of Man

was like the image of Adam his father (not only according to the body, but also according to the soul); rather, the understanding of the words and the literal sense expressly implies that he [Adam] begot a living son, similar to his image in soul, spirit, and body, who namely received the soul as well as the body and spirit from him. For this is also certain according to nature: that no flesh or body can generate and propagate its own kind without having the soul and **astral spirit** <note>The "astral spirit" was a common concept in 17th-century alchemy and theology, referring to a "star-like" or ethereal body that mediated between the immortal soul and the physical flesh.</note> united within it. Therefore, it is impossible for the devil and spirits—even if they take a dead human body upon themselves and through it mix carnally with a living woman, as often happens <note>A reference to the folklore of "incubi," demons believed to take physical form to consort with humans.</note>—to propagate or beget a living fruit, because they have no human souls in them. 

In a living man, however, there is such a soul which, during <term>sexual union</term> <note>German: *Beyschlaff*.</note>, again emits and, as it were, sprays out glowing **soul-sparks**. These same sparks are surrounded by a small mass of human blood (in which they lie hidden <note>original Latin: *latitiren*</note> like a fire-spark in the ashes) and are thus, with and in the man’s seed and blood, effectively sunk into the **matrix** <note>original Latin: *matricem*; a formal term for the womb.</note> or **womb** of the woman in a propagative and communicative manner. 
<margin>Leviticus 17:11, 14.</margin>
Therefore, the Holy Scripture says: *The soul of all flesh is in the blood* <note>original Latin: *Anima omnis carnis in Sanguine est*</note>; that is, the soul of every kind of flesh is in the blood. Likewise: *Their blood...*

<summary>This page argues that human procreation involves the transmission of the soul and "astral spirit" from the father to the child, using the soul's presence in the blood as a primary justification. It contrasts human life with demons, who can mimic the physical act of sex but cannot create life because they lack a human soul.</summary>

<page-num>110</page-num>
## On Passing Away and Departure,

It was stated in the [previous] chapter that when a person dies, his soul or spirit travels out and goes forth from him. Now, someone might further ask: How exactly must such a going forth or traveling out of the soul or spirit take place? Or what are the circumstances surrounding such a departure of the spirit?

Regarding this, we must remember what we cited in the 20th Chapter: that the soul or spirit in a human being is a **burning little flame** that flames and burns in the heart, like a light in a lamp. When it happens that either through natural causes this same little soul-flame lacks its nourishment <note>original: "fovrung," referring to the fuel or vital heat that sustains life</note> (so that it no longer has that by which it can maintain itself in its **heart-lamp**), or also when through violent causes that same little soul-flame is dampened, smothered, or blown upon by an external air, then it goes out and is extinguished. And this is precisely what is meant when it is otherwise said that a person's soul or spirit has traveled out or gone forth.

The Apostle **James** looked toward this <margin>James 4:14.</margin> when he wrote: Our life is a vapor <note>The author uses "Dampf" (steam/vapor) to describe the fragility of life</note> that lasts for a little time, but afterwards it vanishes. And where it stands in the Psalm: <margin>Ps. 102:4.</margin> My days are passed away like smoke, and withered like grass. The wise man **Sirach** also indicates such a mystery in the spirit when he says: *Weep over the dead, for his light has failed* <note>original Latin: *Super mortuum plora, deficit enim lux ejus*</note>: You may mourn over a dead person, for he no longer has his light. As the Prophet **Jeremiah** in his...
<margin>Sirach 22:10.</margin>

<meta>catchword: ...his (nen)</meta>

<summary>This page explains the "departure" of the soul by using the metaphor of a flame in a lamp (the heart) being extinguished, either through a lack of fuel or external force. It supports this imagery with biblical references from James, the Psalms, and Sirach.</summary>
<keywords>soul as flame, heart-lamp, departure of the spirit, James 4:14, Psalm 102, Sirach, nature of death</keywords>

--- Page 119 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical and theological comparison of the human soul to a lamp's flame. <page-num>111</page-num>
<header>[Extinguishing] of Souls.</header>

<margin>Lamentations 2:11, 12.</margin>
...in his Lamentations, when he remembers the little children and infants of the Israelites (how their souls went out in their mothers' bosoms), he uses these words: *When they breathed out their souls.* <note>original Latin: "Cum exhalarent animas suas." The author is connecting the act of breathing out—exhaling—with the flickering out of a lamp.</note> Which word *exhalare* actually means as much as when a light-flame goes out, is extinguished, or is blown out. This is what **Elihu** means when he speaks: <margin>Job 36:32.</margin> *In his hands God hides the light and commands it that it must return and appear again:* <note>original Latin: "In manibus suis Deus abscondit lucem & præcipit ei, ut rursus adveniat"</note> that is: **God** hides the light in his hands and then commands it to return and appear once more. <margin>Ps. 23:4 and throughout; Psalm 107:10, 14; Isaiah 9:2; Jeremiah 13:16; Luke 1:79.</margin> Therefore, death is called *darkness and shadow* <note>original Latin: "tenebræ & umbra"</note> in the Scripture.

But regarding **Christ’s** Divine Soul, it is not the intention that in His death it should have been extinguished in the same way (as otherwise happens with all other human beings). The Apostle and Evangelist **John** testifies to this when he writes: <margin>John 1:4, 5.</margin> *In Christ was life, and the life was the light of men, and the light shines in the darkness, and the darkness has not understood it:* Therefore the prophet **Isaiah** also (when **He** remembers the death of the **Lord** **Christ**) uses these words, <margin>Isaiah 53:10</margin> *to lay down his soul,* <note>original Latin: "ponere animam suam"</note> that is, to set his soul aside. Indeed, the **Lord** **Christ** himself uses those very same words when he speaks: <margin>John 10:17, 18.</margin> *I lay down my soul, that I may take it again: No one takes it from me, but I lay it down of myself: I have the power to lay it down, and the power to take it again.* <note>original Latin: "Pono animam meam, ut iterum sumam eam : Nemo tollit eam à me, sed ego pono eam à me ipso : potestatem habeo ponendi eam, & iterum sumendi eam."</note> That is: I lay my soul away, so that I may take it back: No one takes

<meta>The catchword "nimbt" (takes) indicates the start of the next page's first sentence: "No one takes it from me..."</meta>

<summary>This page explains the metaphor of death as the extinguishing of a lamp (exhalation), while distinguishing Christ's death as a voluntary laying down of life rather than a forced loss of the soul's light.</summary>

-># Of Surrendering and Departure,<-

takes it <note>Referring to his soul/life from the previous page.</note> away from me / but I lay it down of my own accord: I have power to lay it down / and I have power to take it up again. ## Chapter XXIX.

->Of our inheritance, which we are to expect from GOD our Father.<-

Since it has been explained above how Almighty God is the Father of us all (according to the soul), we have, accordingly, a magnificent and distinguished 
<margin>Revelation 21:7</margin> inheritance to look forward to from the aforementioned Heavenly Father: namely, that we shall inherit all things. This inheritance was indeed possessed by our first parents before the Fall, 
<margin>Wisdom 2:23</margin> as they were created for eternal life; but through the fall of sin, they were 
<margin>Isaiah 59:2</margin> separated from GOD and lost that same inheritance. However, through the Son of God (who was 
<margin>Galatians 4:4 and following</margin> sent into the world for this purpose and born of a woman), we receive once again the status of children. Accordingly, we should know that we shall receive from the <term>Lord</term> <note>original German: "HErrn"</note> the 
<margin>Colossians 3:24</margin> **repayment of the inheritance** <note>original Latin: "Retributionem hæreditatis"</note>; that is, the compensation or the handing back of the ancestral portion. <margin>Psalm 142:6</margin> inheritance to me." And again: "Lord, You are my portion in the land of the living."

now heard that Christ, the Son of **God**, is the Mediator <term>Mediator: A go-between who reconciles two parties; here, Christ is the bridge between the Divine and humanity.</term> and the means through which we are reunited with **God** and are to regain life. But one must know this in particular: that on the Last Day <note>The Day of Judgment or the end of the world.</note>, neither **God** nor His Son shall create or make entirely new souls for us. For if that were the intention, it would not have been necessary for Christ, the Son of **God**, to become man, suffer, die, and rise again; much less would He need to have any communion <note>original: *Gemeinschafft*; refers to a shared spiritual fellowship or participation.</note> with human souls in this life. Rather, if the **LORD God** were of the mind that He wished to wipe out both body and soul in man, He could, after the destruction of this current world and humanity, not only create an entirely new human being through His Word once again, but also renew them completely, breathing a new soul out of His Divine Essence, and thus allow newly created children of men and descendants to propagate again. 

But such is not the intention at all. Therefore, we mentioned above in Chapter 28 that the human soul, though it may be extinguished like a flame at a person’s death, is nevertheless not fundamentally wiped out or killed. 
<margin>Luke 9:56</margin>
The **Lord** Christ looked toward this very point when He said: He did not come to destroy the souls of men, but to preserve <note>original: *erhalten*; meaning to keep, sustain, or save from ruin.</note> them.

## Chapter XXXVI.

->Of the Second and Eternal Death, to which only the Damned are Subject.<-

<margin>Psalm 89:49, Sirach 8:8, Romans 5:12, Hebrews 9:17</margin> That which is primarily and properly (so to speak) called "death" is distinguished by Holy Scripture in this way: it usually calls one the "first death" and the other the "second death." Now, concerning the first death (which is appointed for the body, and which we discussed above in Chapter 26), all people must die without distinction; but <margin>Revelation 2:11 & chapter 20:6</margin> from the second death (which is appointed for the soul), the chosen <term>Außerwehlten; the elect or those chosen by God for salvation</term> are freed, so that this same death has no power over them. Therefore the Lord Christ says: <margin>John 11:25 and chapter 8:51</margin> Whoever believes in me shall live, even if he were to die; and whoever lives and believes in me shall never—

That all of this is indeed true and certain (as was noted above in the preceding chapter), daily experience further testifies to the fact that our flesh and blood dies here in this world and becomes earth, dust, and ashes. For as it was explained above how unclean and sinful a thing our flesh and blood is, so this in turn is the wages of sin <note>The German "Sünden Sold" refers to the biblical concept from Romans 6:23 that death is the "payment" or natural consequence earned by human sinfulness.</note> (or the punishment we have deserved through it), that our flesh must thus die and perish. As the Apostle Peter also writes: That the dead are judged according to man in the flesh. For because all people have sinned, death is therefore ordained over all flesh, so that all people without distinction must die.

Therefore, the pious Joshua also said when he felt his death approaching: "Behold, I enter the way of all flesh" <note>original: *Ecce, ingredior viam universæ carnis*</note>, which is to say: See, I am going the way that all flesh must go. For as the wise man <note>Likely referring to Jesus son of Sirach, the author of the Book of Ecclesiasticus mentioned in the margin.</note> says: All flesh wears out like a garment; and Isaiah (as well as others) has said: That all flesh is like hay <note>Often translated as "grass" in English Bibles (Isaiah 40:6).</note>, and even at its best, it is like a flower.

# Of the New Birth.

...should proceed and happen in such a manner, like a dew falling from heaven out of the morning-dawn; therefore it is all the less to be wondered at, because our Savior’s supernatural <term>conception or generation</term> <note>The author uses both the Latinate *Conceptio* and the German *Erzeugung* to describe the Virgin Birth.</note> (which otherwise in the New Testament is called the <margin>Luke 1:35.</margin> **overshadowing of the power of the Most High**) occurred in such a manner. This liturgical text, often called the "Rorate," traditionally associates the dew of heaven with the incarnation of Christ.</note>

Such a heavenly manner, then (to give birth through the light dew falling from heaven out of the morning-dawn), is so exceedingly great a mystery that it is indeed entirely impossible for a <term>natural man</term> <note>A "natural man" refers to a person living only by their physical senses and reason, without spiritual enlightenment.</note> to grasp, conceive, or understand it. In the same way, one cannot express it or adequately describe it with any physical words; rather, only a spiritually minded person, enlightened by God, can to some extent grasp such a supernatural mystery through his God-enlightened understanding.

# That the Chosen Will Receive New...

even the rotting of the grain in the earth is not enough on its own for it to sprout forth anew; rather, the earth must also be moistened by the dew and the rain. The grain, too (which you sow, as Paul says again in that place), is not the new fruit or the **body** <note>original: "*Corpus*"</note> that is to come from it, but a bare grain. Yet the Lord GOD gives life back to it (even though it is already rotten and dead) through the moistening of the earth, or the <term>vegetative soul <note>original: "*animam vegetativam*"; in the science of the time, this was the lowest level of the "soul" or life-force that allowed plants to grow and reproduce.</note></term> (as the scholars call it). By this means, its own previously rotten **matter** <note>original: "*materia*"</note> greens forth again, and from it a beautiful, tall ear of corn grows. In its shape and form, as it stands there in the field, this ear is not the same as the previous grain (which was cast into the earth), much less is it exactly the same grain; rather, it is much more magnificent, beautiful, lively, and lovely to look upon than the dry grain could ever be in itself.

<margin>On which see above, Ch. 29. Wisdom; Ezekiel 37:5, 6; Isaiah 66:14</margin>

Whoever now looks upon this mystery, presented by the highly enlightened Apostle Paul, with truly spiritual eyes—reflecting upon it deeply in the fear of God—will see how the parables compare to the burying of a dead body into the earth: its rotting, its moistening by the **heavenly dew from the morning-redness**, the breathing back into life, and the restoration of breath; its (the body's) sprouting forth again or greening, its magnificent improvement, and its other renewed form.

This happens now (as previously mentioned) through water and Spirit, and thus through the purification and sanctification; both of these must be rooted and begun within every person while still here in this mortal life (whoever intends to attain eternal life). However, for any person in whom this does not occur, no physical seed or balsam remaining in their bones will help them toward the resurrection of eternal life, just as little as 

<margin>
See what is contained in 4 Esdras 8:41 and following. <note>original Latin: "Vide quæ habentur 4 Esdr. 8. v. 41. & seq."</note>
</margin>
a completely hollow grain can grow back into an ear of corn in the earth (in a temporal and earthly manner). For because it no longer possesses a <term>vegetative soul</term> <note>original Latin: "animam vegetativam"; in early philosophy and biology, this was the "soul" or power of life responsible for growth, nutrition, and reproduction</note> within itself, it remains lying in the soil and brings forth no fruit.

Therefore, it is unavoidably necessary that every person (who thinks to rise again to eternal life) must truly and essentially obtain that same spiritual seed while still here in this life, as we intend to prove and demonstrate here through the testimonies of the Word of God. The Scripture gives to that same spiritual seed...

<summary>The author argues that physical preservation of the body is insufficient for resurrection; a spiritual "seed" must be planted in the soul during one's lifetime to replace the "evil seed" of the Fall. He uses the analogy of a hollow grain, which cannot sprout without its vital life-force, to explain why sanctification is necessary for the afterlife.</summary>

Of the Bodies of the Elect.

## Chapter XLVI.
### Regarding the Substance from which these New Bodies of the Elect shall be formed.

<margin>1 Corinthians 15:40 & following, throughout.</margin>
Whenever we compare the first birth or creation of humanity with the second new birth or rebirth, and thus weigh the future against the past (as the Apostle Paul is accustomed to do), we will always be better directed toward the new birth or rebirth (as a future state) and be able to grasp and understand it more clearly. When we look at the first generation or creation of man from the Word of God, Scripture says:
<margin>Genesis 2:7.</margin>
that **GOD** made the first man, Adam, from a clod of earth and breathed the living breath into his nostrils. Thus, man was at that time joined together and made from earth and spirit. Paul calls this the 
<margin>1 Corinthians 2:14, 15.</margin>
<term>Natural Man <note>original German: "Natürlichen Menschen" — This refers to humanity in its physical, mortal state, governed by natural biological laws rather than divine spirit.</note></term>, who is made for this natural life. However, the man made for the future and spiritual life, 
<margin>1 Corinthians 15</margin>
<margin>Ibid. 15:44.</margin>
He calls *Spiritualem* <note>Latin: "spiritual"</note>, a spiritual man, who shall have a spiritual body. Regarding what such a body shall be composed of, the Royal Prophet David spoke of this in the Old Testament in a mysterious way through the Spirit
<margin>Psalm 147:18.</margin>
when he says: 
> The Lord shall send forth his word, and melt them: he shall blow <note>original Latin: "Emittet Dominus verbum suum, & liquefaciet ea : flabit." This verse is used here to illustrate how God's Word acts as a transformative force upon the "frozen" or material state of man.</note>

<margin>

For just as otherwise, through elemental water, according to its nature and property, every <term>solid body <note>original Latin: *solidum Corpus*</note></term> (whatever it may be in the whole world) is held together in its great substance, so that it remains a whole body or a solid thing; on the contrary, however (when the water is entirely withdrawn from it), that same creature falls apart like ashes into the smallest parts or atoms. 

Thus must those men reborn of water and spirit in eternal life also be held together inseparably in one body for all eternity, which is a very great mystery, and was previously prefigured and indicated to us through the <term>Sacrament of Holy Water-Baptism</term>. <margin>Revelation 15:2.</margin>

At this point, however, Epicurean <note>Followers of Epicurus; here the author uses the term to describe materialists or scoffers who doubt spiritual realities and focus only on physical logic.</note> children of the world and scoffers might easily be found, who would blaspheme and say: "Ah, so I hear it clearly: in that life to come, the elect children of God will have bodies frozen together from water and wind, which could then easily be melted away again by the sun?"

Answer: In that place, there will be no such earthly, unstable, destructible, and fleeting condition for the reborn bodies of the elect. For even in this natural life, though the body of the first man was indeed created out of the earth, it nevertheless does not remain an earthy or earthen matter in either form or substance (which could also have easily been blown into heaps by the wind), but instead becomes flesh, bone, and blood. And this is especially heart-felt and comforting, that the aforementioned spirit-filled Apostle **Paul** gives us clearly to understand—in the very same epistle and the chapter cited next—that such a great **mystery** (which is prefigured for us through these two **sacraments** of **Holy Baptism** and the **Supper** in the **New Testament**) was also spiritually indicated to the **Old Fathers** <note>original: *Alt-Vätern* — referring to the Patriarchs or ancestors of faith in the Hebrew Bible</note> in the **Old Testament**.

# Of Bodies in General.

...but it is also, according to nature, quite artfully indicated and prefigured for us through such a **metaphor** <note>original: *Gleichniß* — can mean parable, likeness, or metaphor. Here it refers to the vine and branches imagery from John 15.</note> how we must have and receive all our nourishment and sustenance (for the conservation and preservation of eternal life) from **Christ** in that world, no less than the vine branches obtain sap and power from their **vine** and grow into a physical fruit, of which we have spoken at length in the chapter immediately preceding.

Now we must come to the two **sacraments** of **Baptism** and the **Holy Supper** <note>original: *H. Nachtmals* — literally the "Holy Night-Meal," referring to the Eucharist or Lord's Supper.</note>; although these have remained and been preserved among all **sects** <note>The author likely refers to the various denominations emerging from the Reformation, such as Lutherans, Calvinists, and Anabaptists.</note> (which merely bear a Christian name) not without special reasons and great **mystery**, yet until this very day only the smallest part (among them all) has looked to what is actually indicated and prefigured for us in **Holy Scripture** through these two sacraments of **Holy Baptism** and the **Supper**. 

Namely, not only that we must be born again to **eternal life** through **water and Spirit** <margin>Of which [we spoke] above in chapter 43. <note>original Latin: *De quo supra cap. 43.*</note></margin>, and that the **Lamb of God** (**Jesus Christ**) will feed, give drink to, nourish, and preserve us there in that world to eternal life with his **flesh and blood** (of which [we spoke] above in chapter 49 <note>original Latin: *de quo supra capite 49.*</note>): but also especially this exceedingly great **mystery** of the **union** of our bodies in the future eternal life. This we treat in this chapter; which the **Apostle** then quite heartily explains regarding both such sacraments, where he first speaks of **Baptism** al-
->so<-

<summary>The author explains that the sacraments of Baptism and the Lord's Supper are not merely rituals, but "mysteries" that prefigure the physical and spiritual union of believers with Christ in the eternal afterlife. He compares this connection to the way branches receive life-giving sap from a vine.</summary>

# Of Bodies in General.

<margin>1 Corinthians 12:12, 13.</margin>
...so writes [Paul]: For just as a body is one, and yet has many members, but all the members of one body (though they are many) are yet one body; so also is **Christ**. For through one Spirit we are all baptized into one body.

<margin>Chapter 10:16, 17.</margin>
And in this same epistle <note>referring to the First Epistle to the Corinthians</note>, he writes of the **Lord’s Supper** <note>original: *Abendmal* — literally "Evening Meal," the standard German term for the Eucharist.</note> thus: The bread that we break is one bread, so we many are one body, because we all partake of one bread.

<margin>See above, chapter 23. <note>original Latin: *vide supra cap. 23.*</note></margin>
It is as if he meant to say: Just as a single and whole thing exists, and is afterwards broken into many pieces, divided, and distributed to so many different people in the Supper; so also do the bodies of all men originate from a single body (namely, that of the first **Adam**), and will ultimately become a single body again, namely in the second **Adam**, who is **Christ**. And this is especially heartfelt and comforting: that the aforementioned Spirit-filled <note>original: *Geistreiche* — literally "rich in spirit," used here to mean divinely inspired.</note> Apostle **Paul**, in this same epistle and in the chapter cited next, gives us to understand explicitly that such a great **mystery** (which is prefigured for us through these two **sacraments** of **Holy Baptism** and the **Supper** in the **New Testament**) was also spiritually indicated to the **Patriarchs** <note>original: *Alt-Vätern* — literally "Old Fathers," referring to the ancestors of the faith in the Old Testament.</note> in the **Old Testament**.

<margin>1 Corinthians 10:2, 3, 4.</margin>
For thus he writes: I would not have you ignorant, dear brothers, that our fathers were all baptized under **Moses**, with the cloud and with the sea; and they all ate the same spiritual food, and they all drank the same spiritual drink; for they drank from the spiritual **Rock** that followed them, which is **Christ**.
